Module 5 — Becoming an Engine · Lesson 5.4

Handles: Generational Indices

Every resource in this engine is passed around as a pointer, a reference, or a std::span, and that works for exactly one reason: nothing is ever destroyed. Three different files say so in their own comments, each admitting the same thing — that they are correct by accident of lifetime rather than by design. Next lesson's asset system can unload, and the moment it can, every one of those pointers becomes a question nobody can answer. Today we replace them with two integers that can answer it, derive the design from the three ways a reference goes wrong, and find out what it costs — in bytes, in nanoseconds, and in one honest failure the design buys down rather than eliminates.

Time
≈ 5 hours
Prereqs
2.12 — Wireframe Meshes, 3.5 — A Hand-Rolled OBJ Loader, 4.8 — Porting the Scene, 5.1 — The Refactor, 5.3 — Logging, Assertions, and Errors
Files
engine/include/engine/core/handle.hpp, engine/include/engine/core/pool.hpp, engine/include/engine/gfx/mesh.hpp, engine/src/gfx/mesh.cpp, engine/include/engine/gfx/scene.hpp, engine/include/engine/gfx/soft_renderer.hpp, engine/src/gfx/soft_renderer.cpp, demos/common/demo_scene.hpp, demos/common/demo_scene.cpp, demos/hello_cube/main.cpp, demos/sandbox/main.cpp
Milestone
Geometry is owned in one place and referred to by a four-byte handle. A stale reference is detected and counted instead of dereferenced; the storage may move, grow and compact underneath it. Golden still byte-identical.

By the end of this lesson you will be able to…

  • …name the three distinct ways a reference into a container goes wrong, and say which designs survive which.
  • …explain why replacing a pointer with a bare index fixes one failure and makes another one worse.
  • …derive the generation counter from the ABA problem rather than being told about it.
  • …choose a bit budget for a handle with arithmetic instead of a shrug, and state what the chosen budget does not protect you from.
  • …implement a generational pool with dense storage, O(1) removal by swap-and-patch, and an occupancy flag that costs nothing.
  • …use a phantom template parameter to make two handle types uninterchangeable at compile time.
  • …state the one rule that keeps a handle-based API honest — resolve late, use immediately, never store — and recognise the code that breaks it.
  • …argue what handles buy beyond safety: relocatable storage, trivial serialization, and a reference small enough to put in a component.

1The Problem

This engine already knows what is wrong with it. Not in a design document — in its own source, in three separate files, written by us at three different times, each admitting the same thing without quite naming it.

Here is hello_cube, our smallest program, explaining why one of its members is a member:

Read the second clause again: the data must outlive every frame that uses it. That is not a design. It is a promise, enforced by nothing, checked by nobody, and true only for as long as this file stays ninety-six lines long. Nothing in the type system knows about it. Move that line into a helper function one afternoon and the program is broken in a way that compiles cleanly, runs, and usually looks fine.

Here is the demo's model loader, which goes further and names the hazard outright:

And here is Lesson 4.8's mesh cache, which is the most damning of the three, because it contains a workaround rather than a comment:

Five comparisons to answer one question — is this the same mesh I saw last frame? — and four of them exist only because the first one cannot be trusted. An address is not an identity. It is a location, and locations get reused.

Every one of these three has survived so far because of a single fact that will not hold much longer: nothing in this engine is ever destroyed. The cube is a member of main's app object. The built-in shapes are inline constexpr arrays with program lifetime. The floor and the loaded model are locals of a function that runs until the window closes. There is no unload, no eviction, no level change, and no streaming. A dangling pointer needs something to be freed, and nothing is.

Lesson 5.5 builds the asset system. An asset system that cannot unload is a lookup table with a grand name, so it will unload — and the instant it does, every borrowed pointer in this codebase becomes a question:

The question

Is this still valid? A pointer cannot answer it. Not by being careful, not with an assertion, not with a debugger. A pointer to freed memory holds exactly the same bits it held when the memory was alive, and those bits will happily name whatever moved in afterwards. From the pointer's side, nothing happened.

So we need a reference that can answer. This lesson builds it, converts one subsystem to it end to end, and proves the conversion changed nothing observable — the same discipline Lessons 5.1, 5.2 and 5.3 each used, and for the same reason: a refactor's whole claim is that nothing changed, and a claim with no way to fail is not a claim.

2Building Intuition

2.1The cloakroom ticket

You hand your coat to a theatre cloakroom and get a small numbered ticket. The ticket is not your coat. It is not a description of your coat, and it does not get bigger if your coat is a parka. It is a number, and it works because the cloakroom keeps a peg for every number.

Notice what the ticket lets the cloakroom do that a "here is exactly where I hung it" note would not. The attendant can move your coat to a different peg, shuffle everything to make room for a coach party, or rebuild the entire rail during the interval — and your ticket is still correct, because it names a relationship the cloakroom maintains rather than a location you were told about once.

Now the part that turns this from a nice analogy into an engineering design. Suppose you lose your ticket, someone finds it, collects your coat, and later a different customer is handed the same numbered ticket for peg 17. You find your old ticket in a pocket and present it. A naive cloakroom hands you a stranger's coat with complete confidence — the number is valid, peg 17 has something on it, everything checks out.

A real cloakroom does not have this problem, because a real ticket has two things printed on it: a peg number and something identifying this visit — a date, a colour for the day, a serial. The attendant checks both. Peg 17 currently holds visit 4; your ticket says visit 3; you are politely told that your coat is not here. That second number is the entire idea of this lesson, and everything below is the same thought expressed in thirty-two bits.

2.2The question a pointer cannot answer

Figure 1 puts the two side by side and asks each of them the only question that matters.

The asymmetry is total, and it is worth being precise about why, because "pointers are dangerous" is folklore and this is not. A pointer is a value whose meaning depends on a fact that is not stored anywhere in it: whether the allocation it names is still the allocation it was created against. That fact lives in the allocator, in the container, in the program's control flow — everywhere except in the sixty-four bits you are holding. So the check you would like to write cannot be written, because the value you would check is not there to check.

A handle stores that fact. Not the whole fact — it stores a fingerprint of it, a small number that changes whenever the answer changes. And a small number that changes is all a check needs.

3The Theory

3.1Three failures, in order

We are going to derive the design rather than be handed it, and the way to derive it is to enumerate what can go wrong. There are exactly three ways a reference into a container stops meaning what it meant, and they are genuinely different from one another — different causes, different symptoms, different fixes.

Failure 1 — dangling. The object was destroyed. The memory it occupied has been returned to the allocator, or the vector holding it was cleared. This is the one everybody knows. Its symptom is the friendliest of the three: reading freed memory often crashes, and a crash is a bug that reports itself. Often, though, is not always — freed memory frequently still contains the old bytes, so the program reads correct-looking data and works fine until the day something else is allocated there.

Failure 2 — aliasing. The object was destroyed and something else took its place. The memory is live, well-formed, and belongs to a different object of the same type. There is no crash to hope for. The program reads a perfectly valid mesh — just not yours. In concurrent programming this pattern has a name, the ABA problem: you look at a location, it says A; you look again, it says A; and in between it went A → B → A, so both observations were true and your conclusion was false.

Failure 3 — relocation. Nothing was destroyed at all. The container grew, reallocated, and moved every element to a new address. Your pointer names memory that is no longer part of the container. This one is peculiarly nasty because nobody did anything wrong: appending to a std::vector is not a suspicious act, and the code that invalidated your pointer may be in a different file written by a different person for an unrelated reason.

Figure 2 is the whole derivation in one picture: three failures down the side, three candidate designs across the top.

3.2The index alone, and why it is worse

The obvious first move is to stop storing an address and store a position instead. Keep the meshes in one array; a reference becomes "element 17". This is a real improvement and it is where a lot of code stops.

Look at what it fixes. Relocation is gone entirely. The vector can grow, reallocate and move every byte it owns; element 17 is still element 17, because an index names a position in a sequence rather than a location in memory. That is a genuine and permanent win, and it is the reason indices show up in engine code long before generations do.

Look at what it does to dangling. Element 17 of an array with 12 elements is out of range, which a bounds check catches, so the easy half of dangling becomes detectable. Good.

Now look at aliasing, and here is the turn. Before, an aliased pointer at least had a chance of pointing at freed memory and crashing. With an index, there is no memory to be freed and nothing to crash: slot 17 was emptied, slot 17 was refilled, and your index now names a live, valid, well-formed object that has nothing to do with what you asked for. A bare index makes failure 2 strictly worse, by converting a probable crash into a guaranteed silent wrong answer.

Why this matters more than it sounds

This is not a theoretical concern in an engine. Slot reuse is not an unlucky accident — it is the design. Pools recycle slots deliberately, because recycling is what makes them cheap. So the aliasing case is the common case: unload a level, load the next one, and every slot in the pool is reused within a second. Any scheme that relies on slots not being reused has picked a fight with its own container.

3.3The generation, derived

So the index is right and incomplete. It answers where, and we need one more piece of information: which occupant. The cloakroom's answer was a second number that changes with each visit; ours is the same, and it needs exactly two properties.

  1. It must change whenever the occupant changes. Otherwise it does not distinguish occupants, which is its only job.
  2. It must be storable in the handle and in the slot. Otherwise there is nothing to compare.

The smallest thing satisfying both is a counter. Give every slot a counter that starts at one and increments each time the slot is vacated. A handle records the counter's value at the moment it was issued. Resolution compares the two:

\[ \text{valid}(h) \;=\; \big(h_{\text{index}} < N\big) \;\wedge\; \text{occupied}\big(h_{\text{index}}\big) \;\wedge\; \big(h_{\text{gen}} = \text{gen}[h_{\text{index}}]\big) \] valid(h) = (index < slot count) AND (slot is occupied) AND (handle generation == slot generation)

Three tests, and each rejects one of the three failures. Let us push a real sequence through it by hand, because the mechanism is small enough that seeing every number removes all doubt.

A worked sequence of pool operations, showing the slot's generation, the handle issued, and what an older handle resolves to.
Operationslot 0 generationhandle issued get(first)
insert(torus)1first = gen 1, idx 0the torus ✓
remove(first)2—nullptr — 1 ≠ 2
insert(cube)2second = gen 2, idx 0nullptr — 1 ≠ 2
remove(second)3—nullptr — 1 ≠ 3

Note row three, which is the entire point of the design. The slot is live. The index is in range. A bare index would have returned the cube with total confidence. The generation mismatch is the only thing standing between you and somebody else's geometry.

Note also when the counter is incremented: on removal, not on insertion. That is the detail that makes the scheme work with no extra state. Bump on removal and a freed slot immediately holds a generation that no outstanding handle carries, so the interval during which the slot is empty is already covered — and the next insertion can hand out the bumped value unchanged. Bump on insertion instead and there is a window, between the free and the next allocation, in which the old handle still matches; you would need a separate "is this occupied" flag to cover it, and a flag you have to remember to check is a bug waiting for a hurried afternoon.

3.4The bit budget, with numbers

Two numbers in one handle means splitting a word, and how you split it is a real design decision with a real trade. Do the arithmetic; do not pick by feel.

We take 32 bits, split 20 index / 12 generation. Twenty bits of index gives 1,048,576 slots, which is more meshes than any scene this course will build and more entities than the ECS in Lesson 5.8 will ever hold; something else in the engine breaks long before a million of anything. Twelve bits of generation gives values 0 to 4,095, of which we reserve zero, leaving 4,095 usable.

Reserving zero is not a rounding error, it is a feature that costs one value and buys something you would otherwise write code for. A live slot's generation is never zero, so a handle whose generation field is zero can never match anything — which means an all-bits-zero handle is the null handle. And that means a value-initialised member, a default-constructed std::vector<handle>, and a struct zeroed by memset are all full of nulls without a line of code or a constructor call.

Now the uncomfortable question: is 4,095 enough? A slot's generation repeats after 4,095 removals of that same slot, and when it repeats, an old handle from the first cycle starts resolving again — to the wrong object. So compute the time, at plausible rates:

Time until one slot's generation repeats, at four recycle rates.
That one slot is recycled…Generations per second Time until it repeats
once per frame at 60 Hz6068 seconds
a hundred times a second10041 seconds
once a second168 minutes
once per level load0.014.7 days

Sixty-eight seconds sounds alarming until you notice what has to happen for it to hurt: a handle must be held, unused, across 4,095 recycles of its own slot and then dereferenced. Holding a reference to an asset you never touch for a minute while that exact asset slot churns every frame is not a shape real code takes. For assets — meshes, textures, sounds, loaded at level boundaries and counted in hundreds — twelve bits is enormous.

For entities in a game spawning and killing thousands of bullets a second it is worth thinking about, and there are two mitigations, neither of which we need today but both of which you should know:

  • A FIFO free list instead of LIFO. We reuse the most recently freed slot, which is warmest in cache — and which concentrates every recycle on one or two slots. Reuse the oldest freed slot instead and the churn spreads across every slot in the pool, multiplying the time-to-wrap by the number of slots. That is the standard fix, and it costs one data structure change.
  • Sixty-four bits, split 32/32. Wrap-around becomes unreachable in any program that will ever run. It also doubles the size of every handle in every component of every entity, which in Lesson 5.8's ECS is a real number of cache lines for a hazard we can measure.

We take the 32-bit split, and — this is the part that turns an assumption into an engineering decision — the pool counts its wraps. If generation_wraps() ever leaves zero, the assumption has been falsified by the program itself and there are two known fixes on the shelf. An assumption nobody measures is a bug with a long fuse.

Not an invention

This exact split — 20 bits of index, 12 of version, in one 32-bit word — is EnTT's default entity layout, and EnTT is the most widely used C++ ECS there is. Bitsquid's engine used a 32-bit id with a similar division. When your arithmetic lands where several independent teams landed, that is worth knowing; when it lands somewhere else, that is worth explaining.

3.5Typed handles, for free

A handle is an integer. Integers are interchangeable, and interchangeable references to different kinds of thing are a mistake generator: pass a texture handle where a mesh handle was wanted and the lookup will succeed — different pool, same integer, and the pool has no idea it was asked the wrong question.

The fix costs nothing at runtime. Make the handle a template whose parameter is never stored:

T appears nowhere in the layout. sizeof(handle<anything>) is 4, the two types are trivially copyable, and — a genuinely useful side effect — T may be incomplete, so a header can declare handle<material> without ever seeing what a material is. What the parameter buys is that the compiler refuses to convert one to the other, so a whole category of mistake becomes a compile error rather than a wrong picture.

This is the same argument Lesson 5.1 made about engine::engine: a distinction the compiler can see costs nothing and catches the mistake before it can run. It is worth naming the pattern, because you will meet it again — a type that exists only to be different from another type is one of the cheapest tools C++ gives you.

3.6What it buys that is not safety

If safety were the only benefit, handles would be a defensive measure — the kind of thing you argue about in code review and skip when you are busy. They are not. Three of the payoffs have nothing to do with correctness, and each one is load-bearing for a later module.

Storage becomes relocatable. Nothing outside the pool holds an address, so the pool may reallocate, reorder, defragment or compact whenever it likes. Every design option that a raw pointer forbids is now open. Our pool exercises this on every single removal, and does it deliberately: §4.4's swap-and-patch moves a live object to a new address while a handle to it keeps working, which is the only real proof that a handle is not just a pointer wearing a costume.

Serialization becomes trivial. A handle is a 32-bit integer with no relationship to any address. Write it, read it, done — no pointer fixups, no relocation tables, no "which base address was this file saved against". Module 8's scene format is a tractable problem largely because of this; a scene graph full of raw pointers is a serious exercise in graph surgery, and a scene graph full of handles is a memcpy.

It is small enough to put in a component. Four bytes, trivially copyable, no lifetime relationship to anything. That is exactly the shape a component in Lesson 5.8's ECS must have — and we can quantify it right now, before the ECS exists. engine::scene_object held a mesh, which is four std::spans: eight pointers, 64 bytes. It now holds four. The struct went from 160 bytes to 96, from two and a half cache lines per object to one and a half, and Lesson 5.6 is entirely about why that sentence is a performance statement and not a tidiness one.

And the cost, stated plainly

A handle is meaningless without its pool. A pointer carries its own context; a handle carries half of one, and the other half has to be reachable at the point of use. So collect_triangles grows a parameter — the pool — and so does every other function that draws a scene_object. That is a real API cost, it is paid at every call site, and §4.7 argues why we pay it in the open rather than hiding it behind a global.

4Implementation

4.1The handle

Start with the layout constants, because every other line in the file is derived from them and putting them first means the split is stated once rather than open-coded five times.

The static_assert is not decoration. Change one constant to experiment with the budget — Exercise 9.2 asks you to — and the assertion fires at compile time rather than letting the two fields overlap and produce handles that alias in a way that would take a very long evening to find.

The handle itself is a struct with one member and four accessors. It has no invariant to defend, so the member is public: a handle is a value, and Module 8's serializer wants exactly that word.

C++ in place — = default on a comparison, and why it matters here

C++20 lets you ask the compiler to generate operator== memberwise. For a one-member struct that is a single 32-bit compare, which is the cheapest possible implementation and also the correct one — two handles are equal exactly when they name the same occupant of the same slot.

Writing it by hand would be four lines that do the same thing until the day somebody adds a member and forgets one of them. The friend keyword makes it a non-member found by argument-dependent lookup, which is the modern idiom: it treats both operands symmetrically and it is what =default is designed for.

Note also explicit on operator bool. Without it, h1 + h2 would compile — two handles quietly converting to bool and adding up to 2. explicit keeps if (h) working and makes the nonsense a compile error.

And the packing primitive. Note that it masks rather than rejects out-of-range input: the range check belongs to whoever is allocating slots, and mixing the two would mean this function needed a way to fail.

Push a number through it by hand. Slot 2, generation 3: the generation shifts left by twenty to become 0x0030_0000, the index contributes 0x0000_0002, and the handle is 0x0030_0002 — 3,145,730 as an integer, which is a perfectly useless number and exactly why index() and generation() exist. Reading it back: bits & 0x000F_FFFF is 2, and bits >> 20 is 3.

4.2The pool: three arrays

Now the container. There is a fifteen-line version of this — one array of items indexed directly by slot, plus an array of generations — and it fixes all three failures. We are not going to write it, and the reason is worth having in front of you before the code.

A slot-indexed array is sparse. Free slots are holes in it. Iterating means walking every slot and testing each one, touching cache lines that contain nothing you want, and the ratio of waste to work grows with every object you ever destroyed. Lesson 5.6 is going to measure exactly this and Lesson 5.8's ECS is going to live or die on it, so the right shape is worth thirty extra lines today.

So: three arrays, and one indirection between them.

Read the roles rather than the types. slots_ is stable — one entry per slot ever created, never moved, indexed directly by a handle's index field. items_ is mobile — a plain std::vector of live objects with no holes, which reallocates as it grows and moves everything it owns, and that is a feature rather than a hazard because nothing outside this class holds an address. owners_ is the way back: owners_[d] is the slot that items_[d] belongs to.

And note the small piece of design in slot::dense. It is the item's position and the occupancy flag: k_no_dense means "this slot holds nothing". A separate bool occupied would have been a byte per slot, a second thing to keep in sync, and a second thing to forget to check. One sentinel does both jobs and cannot disagree with itself.

4.3insert, and the free list

Three things in here are decisions rather than mechanics.

The reused slot's generation is not touched. It was bumped on the way out (§4.4), so it already holds a value no outstanding handle carries, and the insert simply hands it out. This is §3.3's "bump on removal" paying off: the free path did the work, so the allocate path has nothing to remember.

Running out of slots returns null, it does not assert. Lesson 5.3 gave us one question for this: could a correct program, on a working machine, encounter this? Loading a scene with a million and one meshes in it is something the world can do to correct code, so it is an error that ships, not an assertion that is compiled out. The caller tests the handle exactly as it would test a failed allocation.

The free list is LIFO. A vector used as a stack is the cheapest structure there is, and the most recently freed slot is the one still warm in cache. The cost is the one §3.4 named: hot allocate/free pairs hammer one slot's generation, which is what makes wrap-around reachable at all. We take the trade and monitor it.

4.4remove: swap and patch

Removal is where a dense container earns its keep and where the interesting bug lives. The dense array must stay packed, so the hole has to be filled — and the only element we can move without disturbing anything else is the last one.

The line marked the patch is the one to stare at. Having moved the last item down into the hole, its old dense position is gone — so the slot that owns it must be told where it lives now, and owners_ is the only way to find that slot without searching. Delete that one line and the pool still compiles, still passes a casual smoke test, and corrupts itself the first time you remove anything other than the last element.

The if (dead != last) guard is not an optimisation; it is correctness. Self-move-assignment (items_[d] = std::move(items_[d])) is not required to leave an object in any particular state, and a std::vector member is entitled to end up empty. Removing the last element without the guard would occasionally be fine and occasionally clear the wrong mesh.

items_[last] = T{} deserves a sentence too. pop_back alone would shrink the vector, but a moved-from mesh_data is not required to have released its four buffers — the standard says "valid but unspecified", and a vector that keeps its capacity is a perfectly valid unspecified state. Assigning a default-constructed T releases it for certain. A pool quietly holding a freed mesh's megabytes is a leak with a clean bill of health.

Removal invalidates more than you removed

The item that moved also changed address. So remove invalidates pointers into the pool generally, not just the one you destroyed. This surprises people, it is unavoidable in any packed container, and it is exactly the class of hazard handles exist to make survivable: the moved item's handle is untouched, because its slot was patched.

4.5contains, get, and the one rule

Three tests, and §3.3 said which failure each one rejects. The range test rejects a fabricated handle or one from a pool with fewer slots. The dense test rejects a slot that is currently free. The generation test rejects the interesting case — a slot that is live again with a different occupant. The null handle needs no special case: its generation is 0 and no live slot ever carries 0.

The one rule

Resolve late, use immediately, never store. The T* that get() returns is valid until the next insert or remove and not one instruction longer. Caching one across a call that mutates the pool re-creates precisely the bug this whole file was written to abolish — with the extra insult that it now looks like modern, handle-based code.

That rule has a consequence worth pointing at, because it is the thing that makes handles cheap as well as safe. get() costs about 0.13 ns more than a dereference (§5). Do it once per object per frame and it disappears into the noise; do it once per vertex and you have built something slower for no reason. So the shape of correct code is: resolve at the boundary, work through the view. §4.7 is that sentence applied to the renderer.

4.6Converting the mesh subsystem

We convert one subsystem end to end, as Lesson 5.3 did — meshes, because scene_object::geometry is a borrowed view today and is the thing that breaks first. No new container was needed: pool<T> knows nothing about geometry, so the whole of the mesh side is two aliases and a copy helper.

to_mesh_data is the bridge from Lesson 2.12's inline constexpr arrays to storage the pool controls, and it is a genuine copy: eight positions and thirty-six indices for the cube, 636 bytes for all three built-ins, once at start-up. It would have been possible to let the pool hold views instead — and that would be a pool handing out handles to memory it does not control, which is the problem this lesson is solving rather than a clever way around it. The built-in shapes are the one case that would have been safe to borrow, and they go in the pool anyway, because "this particular mesh is safe to borrow and that one is not" is exactly the distinction a caller should never have to keep in their head.

Then scene_object changes by one word:

On the demo side, the four places that were hand-owning geometry collapse into one type. The demo's mesh_library is three handles, a pool, and a function — deliberately the smallest thing that could be called an asset system, because Lesson 5.5 is where it grows loading, lookup by name and eviction:

And the two rebuild paths — build_floor on T and load_model on L — become the mechanism's showcase. Each one removes the old mesh and inserts the new, so the handle changes:

That is two lines with a design decision inside them. Overwriting in place — resolving the handle and assigning over the mesh_data — would have been shorter and would have kept the handle valid. It would also have made the change invisible, which is convenient right up until something downstream is caching per mesh and never learns that its cache is stale. A new mesh gets a new handle, and §4.8 is the code that benefits.

Note the ordering in load_model, which is the same shape and is not arbitrary: the new geometry is built into a local and only handed to the pool at the very end. The old mesh therefore stays resolvable for the whole of the load, so a load that fails leaves the demo showing what it was showing. Free first and you have committed to the new asset before finding out whether it exists.

4.7The renderer resolves at the boundary

collect_triangles gains one parameter and eight lines. Here is the shape:

Three things are happening in eight lines, and all three are the point of the lesson.

The failure has a location. Before, an object whose geometry had been freed read through a dangling span somewhere inside a per-vertex loop; now it fails on a named line, at the top, before any work. That is the difference between a bug and a symptom.

One missing asset costs one object, never the frame. We continue rather than bail, because a renderer that refuses to draw anything because one mesh is missing is worse than one that draws the rest and says so.

The resolution is hoisted. The old code wrote objects[i].geometry.vertices[v] inside the per-vertex loop. Resolving per vertex would have made handles cost something real — a lookup on the torus's 1,225 vertices instead of one. Resolve at the boundary, work through the view. This is not a micro-optimisation we happened to notice; it is the rule from §4.5 applied at the one place it matters most.

The new parameter deserves a defence, because Lesson 5.1 spent a whole section celebrating collect_triangles going from fifteen parameters to eight, and this takes it to nine.

The parameter is not clutter, it is the cost of the design made visible. A handle is half a reference; the pool is the other half; and a function that resolves handles needs both. The alternatives are worse. A global mesh pool would remove the parameter and replace it with an invisible dependency that cannot be tested, cannot be swapped, and makes two pools impossible — and this lesson has two pools by the end of §4.8. A pool pointer stored inside each handle would make handles twelve bytes and put us back where we started.

Module 5's ECS will eventually fold the pools into a world object that carries them together, at which point this becomes one parameter again. But a context object invented before three callers have asked for one is a guess, and Lesson 5.1's rule stands: let the pressure accumulate until the shape is obvious.

The other half of "one object, not the frame" is that the skip is counted:

Sit with that field for a moment. There was no version of it before this lesson — not a harder one, none. A dangling std::span is not detectable, so an object whose geometry had been freed either drew garbage or crashed, and in neither case was there anything to count. The number exists because the design made the failure observable, and that is what "safety" concretely means here: not that nothing goes wrong, but that when something does, the program knows.

4.8Five tests become two

Finally, back to §1's worst offender. Lesson 4.8's mesh cache compared five things to decide whether it had seen a mesh before, and its own comment admitted that four of them were scaffolding around an address that could not be trusted.

The first test is a single 32-bit equality, and it is correct rather than approximately correct. Rebuild the floor and the handle changes, so the cache misses and re-uploads — which is what you wanted, and which the old key could only achieve by comparing vertex counts and hoping two different meshes never had the same shape.

The cache also gains a second pool, and this is a small design point worth naming. Its entries hold geometry it produced — with_normals output, a different vertex count and a different index buffer from the mesh it came from. That is a mesh in its own right, so it goes in a pool of its own rather than being carried around as a struct member:

meshes holds what was authored or loaded; derived_ holds what an import step made from it. Lesson 5.5 gives that relationship a name and a lifetime policy; today it is enough that both are addressed the same way, and that the software path can draw the derived geometry by being handed meshes.derived() — which, note, is a second pool in the same frame, and therefore the concrete reason §4.7 refused a global.

One more receipt. The upload log now names the handle, so the identity the cache is keying on is visible in the terminal:

Two uploads for a three-object scene, because two of those objects are the same cube at different transforms — the cache working, now on identity rather than on an address that happened to agree.

5What It Cost, and What It Bought

Everything below comes out of scratch/measure_54.py and scratch/verify_54.cpp, so it can be re-derived rather than trusted.

The resolution cost. A microbenchmark walks 4,096 meshes 20,000 times, once through pool.get(handle) and once through a raw const mesh_data* taken beforehand, alternating the two so that a machine which warms up does it to both.

Cost per resolution, handle versus raw pointer, over five runs.
ns per accessfive runs
pool.get(handle)0.980.906 – 0.993
a raw pointer0.850.833 – 0.856
difference0.130.073 – 0.146

A third of a nanosecond is roughly half a cycle on this machine, and the reason it is that cheap is worth understanding rather than celebrating: the two extra loads (slots_[index].generation and .dense) are adjacent in one cache line, and they are on the critical path of a dependent load that is going to miss anyway. The comparisons are free in its shadow.

At one resolution per object per frame — where §4.7 put it — a 4,096-object scene pays 0.48 µs per frame, 0.003% of a 60 Hz budget. At one resolution per vertex it would be a different conversation entirely, which is the whole argument for resolving at the boundary.

What the structs weigh. These are measured with sizeof, and the "before" number is not arithmetic — the harness rebuilds the old struct member for member so the compiler's padding is included.

Sizes before and after the conversion.
BeforeAfter
the reference itselfmesh, 64 Bmesh_handle, 4 B16× smaller
engine::scene_object160 B96 B40% smaller
64-byte cache lines per object2.501.50Lesson 5.6's subject
a 4-object scene array640 B384 B
owning geometry members in demos/60one owner now
the sandbox cache key5 tests2 tests§4.8

The harness. verify_54 runs 68 checks in seven sections and passes identically at -O0 and at -O2 -DNDEBUG. Three of them are the ones this lesson stands or falls on:

And §D, which is the honest one. It recycles a single slot until its generation comes back round, and then shows the failure the bit budget buys down rather than eliminates:

The acceptance tests, all green
  • The golden is byte-identical. Seven pinned frames, hash 905BF27E, 1,209,616 bytes, 0 bytes differ from the reference written in Lesson 5.1 before any of this existed. hello_cube --shot likewise matches its 5.2 reference exactly.
  • A stale handle is detected, not dereferenced — proved by freeing a slot, reallocating it, and showing the old handle fails (§B2), and again through the renderer with a live scene (§F).
  • The pool survives a reallocation that moves every element (§B3) and a removal that moves a live one (§C).
  • The whole tree still builds at -Wall -Wextra with no warnings, and the GPU scene, the software scene and both demos run unchanged.

One thing this lesson deliberately did not do: Lesson 5.3 left 61 bool- returning functions and three status enums unconverted, and they are still unconverted. That is not an oversight. Handles are a big enough idea on their own, and the ECS in 5.7 and 5.8 depends on getting them right rather than on getting them soon.

8Common Pitfalls & Debugging

1 · A cached T*, which is the whole bug coming back

Symptom. Everything works until a level loads, then one object draws garbage — and it is not the object you just loaded.

Cause. Somebody resolved a handle once, stored the mesh_data* in a struct, and used it next frame. insert reallocated; remove moved an item. The pointer is stale and it looks like modern handle-based code, which makes it harder to spot than the pointer it replaced.

Fix. Store the handle, resolve at the point of use. grep -n "mesh_data\*" across your own code and check every result against the rule: resolve late, use immediately, never store. If a hot loop genuinely needs the pointer, hoist it above the loop and take care that nothing inside the loop mutates the pool.

2 · error: explicit qualification required to use member 'handle'

Symptom. Writing handle<mesh_data> h; inside a class that has a member function called handle() — which several of ours do, since gpu_device::handle() and friends return the underlying SDL object — produces a baffling error about a dependent base class.

Cause. Name lookup finds the member first. This is not a hypothetical; it reproduces in four lines.

Fix. Use the alias (mesh_handle) or qualify (engine::handle<mesh_data>). The aliases exist partly for this reason, which is why every line of engine and demo code in this lesson uses one.

3 · Everything resolves, but you are drawing the wrong mesh

Symptom. get() never returns null and the picture is still wrong.

Cause. Almost always a handle stored in two places, one of which was updated on reload and one of which was not — a copy of a scene_object taken before the rebuild, say. The handle is not stale; it is a valid handle to a mesh you no longer mean.

Fix. A handle is a value, so treat it like one: derive it from a single source each frame rather than copying it forward. build_scene is the pattern — it rebuilds the whole object array from the library every frame and never carries a handle across.

4 · remove() corrupts the pool for everything but the last element

Symptom. Remove from the middle, and some other handle starts resolving to the wrong item or to nothing.

Cause. The patch line in §4.4 is missing or wrong: slots_[owners_[dead]].dense = dead;. The moved item's slot still points at where it used to be.

Fix. The test that catches it is §C of the harness — remove from the middle, then check every surviving handle. A smoke test that only ever removes the last element will pass forever with this bug in place.

5 · A handle from the wrong pool

Symptom. A handle that is definitely valid resolves to null, or worse, resolves to something unrelated.

Cause. Two pools of the same type — which §4.8 now has — and the handle was minted by the other one. The type system cannot help: both are mesh_handle.

Fix. Keep the pool and its handles together structurally, as mesh_library and scene_mesh_cache both do. If you have several pools of one type and they are genuinely interchangeable at call sites, a per-pool id compiled into debug builds and checked in contains() is the standard next step — Exercise 9.4.

6 · generation_wraps() is not zero

Symptom. The warning fires: pool: generation wrapped on slot 3.

Cause. One slot has been recycled 4,095 times. Almost always a remove/insert pair in a per-frame path — updating an asset in place by replacing it, once a frame, forever.

Fix. First ask whether the churn is necessary; a mesh that is rebuilt every frame probably wants to be mutated through get(), not replaced. If the churn is real, switch the free list to FIFO (§3.4) or widen the handle. Do not ignore it: it is the one failure mode this design does not close.

9Exercises

  1. 9.1 — Break it on purpose. In demos/hello_cube/main.cpp, add a line to on_start() that removes the cube from the pool immediately after inserting it. Predict what you will see before you run it, then run it. Now do the same experiment against the pre-5.4 code (git stash is your friend) by clearing cube_'s vectors after taking the view.

    Hint

    Ask collect_triangles for its stats — pass a collect_stats and print unresolved.

    Solution sketch

    After: a black window, unresolved == 1, and no crash — the failure is reported and localised. Before: on most machines, a perfectly normal cube, because the freed vector's memory has not been reused yet and still contains the vertices. That second result is the important one. The old code is not broken in a way you can see, which is precisely why it survived four modules and would have survived into a shipped game.

  2. 9.2 — Move the split. Change the bit budget to 24 index / 8 generation and run the harness. Which checks fail, and are they failing because the code is wrong or because the test encoded the old budget? Then compute the new wrap time at 60 Hz and decide whether you would ship it.

    Hint

    Only the two constants at the top of handle.hpp should need touching. If anything else needs touching, that is a finding worth writing down.

    Solution sketch

    §A's explicit 1048576 / 4095 check fails, and §D's "4,095 recycles" fails — both because the test hard-codes the budget, which is the right way round: a test that adapts silently to a changed constant is not testing the constant. Nothing in the pool needs changing, which is the real result. 8 bits gives 255 generations, so one slot recycled at 60 Hz wraps in 4.25 seconds — comfortably reachable by a reload key held down, so no, not for entities. For a pool of at most a few hundred long-lived assets it would be fine, and the freed twelve bits buy nothing anybody needs. That is the shape of the argument either way: what churns, how fast, and for how long is a handle held?

  3. 9.3 — FIFO free list. §3.4 claims that reusing the oldest freed slot multiplies the time-to-wrap by the number of slots. Implement it — a std::deque, or a head/tail index into a ring — and change §D of the harness to prove the claim on a pool of 16 slots.

    Hint

    With N slots in rotation, a given slot is recycled once every N frees, so its generation advances N times more slowly.

    Solution sketch

    free_slots_.front() plus pop_front() on a std::deque is the whole change. With 16 slots the wrap takes 16 × 4,095 = 65,520 recycles instead of 4,095. Now measure insert: LIFO is faster, because the slot you just freed is in L1 and the one you freed 65,000 operations ago is not. That is the trade, and it is a real one — which is why the choice is a comment in the header rather than an accident.

  4. 9.4 — Catch the foreign handle. Pitfall 5 is the one hole the type system does not close: two mesh_pools, and a handle from the wrong one. Close it. Give each pool a small id, steal a few bits or add a debug-only field, and make contains() reject a handle whose pool id does not match.

    Hint

    Stealing bits is the interesting version, because it forces you to say which budget pays for it. A debug-only std::uint16_t alongside is the version you would actually ship, and Lesson 5.3's SDL_ASSERT_LEVEL is how you gate it.

    Solution sketch

    A static counter handing out pool ids, an id stored in each pool, and — in a build with assertions — a parallel std::vector<std::uint16_t> of the id each handle was minted with, checked in contains(). Stealing 4 bits from the generation instead gives 255 generations and 16 pools, which §3.4's arithmetic says is a bad trade for assets. The general lesson: when a check cannot be made free, make it free in release and present in development, which is exactly the shape of ENGINE_ASSERT.

  5. 9.5 — Compact it. §3.6 claims that handles make storage relocatable. Cash the claim: add pool::shrink_to_fit() that releases unused capacity, and then the harder one — a defragment() that reorders items_ into handle-index order so that iteration matches allocation order. Prove with the harness that every outstanding handle still resolves afterwards.

    Hint

    Reordering the dense array means recomputing every slot::dense and every owners_ entry. Sort the dense indices by their owner, then walk once and patch. There is no way to do this at all with raw pointers, which is the point of the exercise.

    Solution sketch

    Build a permutation of [0, size) sorted by owners_[d], apply it to both items_ and owners_, then walk owners_ once setting slots_[owners_[d]].dense = d. Every handle keeps working because no handle ever named a dense index. Whether it is worth doing is a Lesson 5.6 question — the answer depends entirely on whether your iteration order correlates with anything, and measuring is the only way to know.

10Recap & Next

The engine gained a way to refer to things that can be wrong and know it. Concretely: a 32-bit typed handle split 20 index / 12 generation, a generational pool with dense storage and O(1) swap-and-patch removal, and one subsystem — meshes — converted end to end, with scene_object 40% smaller and the sandbox's five-test cache key down to two.

The three failures, one last time, because they are the thing to carry forward and the rest is implementation. Dangling: the object was freed. Aliasing: the object was freed and something took its place — the one a bare index makes worse, and the reason the generation exists. Relocation: nothing was freed, the container just moved. A raw pointer loses all three. An index survives one and detects half of another. An index plus a generation handles all three, in four bytes, for a third of a nanosecond.

And the honest limit, restated so it is not lost: 12 bits of generation is a budget, not a guarantee. 4,095 recycles of one slot and an old handle starts resolving again. We measured the time that takes at four plausible rates, decided it is unreachable for assets, wrote down the two known fixes, and made the pool count its own wraps so the assumption is monitored rather than believed.

Next: 5.5 — The Asset System v1. Everything today was the mechanism; next lesson is the policy. Loading, owning, and finding resources — a mesh you can ask for by name rather than by handle, loaded once however many times it is requested, and unloaded when nothing wants it any more. All three of those questions have been unanswerable until today, for the same reason: you cannot free something safely until you can say what happens to the references. Now you can.

It also inherits a specific piece of unfinished business. scene_mesh_cache now holds two pools, one of loaded geometry and one of geometry derived from it by an import step, with no rule connecting their lifetimes — free the source and the derived mesh lives on, keyed by a handle that no longer resolves. That is not a bug today, because nothing frees. It is exactly the bug 5.5 has to be designed against.

11Further Reading

  • Niklas Frykholm, “Managing Decoupling Part 4: The ID Lookup Table” (Bitsquid blog, 2011). The canonical short piece on this exact structure, from the engine that popularised it. Worth reading for the discussion of the index/generation split in a shipping engine. bitsquid.blogspot.com
  • Sean Middleditch, “Data Structures for Entity Systems: Contiguous Storage” (2013). Where the dense/sparse pairing and the swap-and-patch removal are laid out clearly, and the bridge from this lesson to Lesson 5.7's sparse sets.
  • EnTT — entt/entity/entity.hpp. The 20/12 split we adopted, in production, with a well-commented implementation of the same three tests. github.com/skypjack/entt
  • Allan Deutsch, “C++Now 2017: The Slot Map Data Structure”. A talk-length treatment of the same container under its other common name, including the API decisions we skipped and the ones we took.
  • Andre Weissflog, “Handles are the better pointers” (2018). The strongest short statement of the argument in §3.6 — that the safety is almost a side benefit, and the real wins are relocatability, serialization and size. floooh.github.io
  • Jeff Preshing, “The ABA Problem” — and any treatment of lock-free stacks. §3.1's failure 2 under its original name, from the field that discovered it. The tagged pointer used there is the same trick as our generation, arrived at independently for a completely different reason, which is usually a sign that an idea is right.
  • Game Engine Architecture, Jason Gregory — §16.2 (Runtime Resource Management). Where handles sit in a real engine's resource manager, and what Lesson 5.5 is about to build on top of them.

course: Build a Professional 3D Game Engine (SDL3 + C++20)
version: 1.0
updated: 2026-09-02 (after Lesson 5.4 — 49 of 94 lessons)

conventions:
  handles: A REFERENCE INTO THE ENGINE IS AN INDEX PLUS A GENERATION, NEVER A
        POINTER. engine::handle<T> is ONE 32-BIT WORD split 20 index / 12
        generation (the same split EnTT uses for entt::entity), packed
        generation-high. T is a PHANTOM parameter — nothing of it is stored,
        sizeof is 4 for every T, T may be incomplete — so handle<mesh_data> and
        handle<texture> are DIFFERENT TYPES and a mix-up is a compile error.
        GENERATION 0 IS RESERVED, which makes the null handle all-bits-zero for
        free: a value-initialised member, a default-constructed vector element and
        a memset struct are all null with no code.
        THE THREE FAILURES, and this is the derivation, not a list:
          dangling    the object was freed
          aliasing    …and something took its place (ABA). A BARE INDEX MAKES
                      THIS WORSE — it converts a probable crash into a guaranteed
                      silent wrong answer, which is why the generation exists.
          relocation  nothing was freed; the container moved. An index survives
                      this and a pointer cannot.
        THE GENERATION IS BUMPED ON REMOVAL, never on insertion. A freed slot then
        already carries a value no outstanding handle has, so "is this slot
        occupied" needs no separate flag and no window exists in which a stale
        handle matches.
        THE BUDGET IS MONITORED, NOT ASSUMED. 4,095 usable generations means a
        slot's generation REPEATS after 4,095 frees of that slot — 68 s at 60 Hz,
        4.7 days at one level load per 100 s. Unreachable for assets, worth
        thinking about for entities. pool::generation_wraps() counts it and the
        pool logs a warning; if it ever leaves zero the two fixes are a FIFO free
        list (spreads reuse across all slots, multiplying time-to-wrap by the slot
        count) or 64 bits split 32/32.
        THE ONE RULE FOR USERS: RESOLVE LATE, USE IMMEDIATELY, NEVER STORE. get()
        returns a T* valid until the next insert or remove and not one instruction
        longer. Storing one re-creates the bug the design abolished, wearing
        modern clothes.
        RESOLVE AT THE BOUNDARY, ONCE PER OBJECT — not once per use and never once
        per vertex. Measured: 0.98 ns vs 0.85 ns for a raw dereference, so +0.13 ns
        per resolution; per object per frame that is 0.003% of a 60 Hz budget on a
        4,096-object scene, and per vertex it would be a different conversation.
        A HANDLE IS HALF A REFERENCE; THE POOL IS THE OTHER HALF. Every function
        that resolves one takes the pool as a parameter (collect_triangles went
        8 -> 9 params, and Lesson 5.1's reduction is not being walked back — this
        is the cost of the design, made visible rather than hidden in a global).
        A GLOBAL WAS REFUSED and there is a concrete reason: sandbox has TWO
        mesh_pools in one frame by the end of 5.4.
  logging: TWO AXES, NEVER ONE. A CATEGORY says WHO is speaking (a noun, a part of
        the program); a PRIORITY says HOW MUCH IT MATTERS. Collapsing them — a
        `LOG_ERROR` *category* — destroys the mechanism, because you can then never
        say "errors from the GPU but not from assets".
        FIVE CATEGORIES, based at SDL_LOG_CATEGORY_CUSTOM (never at a literal —
        SDL reserves everything above that enumerator for applications, and a
        number would collide the week SDL adds one):
          log_core  log_platform  log_gfx  log_gpu  log_asset
        A static_assert ties the name table to the enum, so adding one without a
        name does not compile. log_gfx currently has ZERO users: the CPU
        rasterizer never logs. Recorded rather than hidden; Module 6 fills it.
        SIX LEVELS, and the meanings are PROMISES that 92 call sites obey:
          error     the operation did not happen, and the caller is being told so
          warn      something went wrong and WE RECOVERED  <- the whole distinction
          info      a fact worth having on a bug report
          debug     what a subsystem did, once per operation
          trace     per-frame or per-item detail
          critical  the program cannot continue
        THE MECHANICAL TEST between error and warn: DID THE OPERATION HAPPEN?
        WHO LOGS A FAILURE: the DEEPEST point that knows WHY. Callers propagate
        SILENTLY and add only the CONSEQUENCE — the thing they know that the
        callee does not. Chosen over "the caller decides the level" because the
        cost of THIS rule is visible (a recovering caller gets an error line it
        did not deserve, fixable by demoting that call to debug) and the cost of
        the other is invisible (detail lost at every layer boundary).
        THE PAYOFF IS FREE AND THAT IS THE ARGUMENT AGAINST A WRAPPER. SDL's
        documented default table is `app=info,assert=warn,test=verbose,*=error`,
        so every category SDL does not know about — i.e. every one of ours —
        defaults to ERROR. Moving the engine off SDL_LOG_CATEGORY_APPLICATION
        makes it quiet with no configuration and no filtering code. A hand-rolled
        logger would have had to reimplement that and would have got a different
        default.
        DEMOS KEEP SDL_Log ON PURPOSE (122 of them). A demo IS the application;
        APPLICATION/info is exactly what its output is, and it is on by default
        because the person who ran the program asked for it.
        `--log SPEC` works on EVERY program: platform::start reads it first thing,
        and app_runner::init fills in argv when the app left it null. Grammar
        borrowed from SDL's own SDL_LOGGING hint so two things a person might type
        do not need two mental models. `*` means OUR categories only — silencing
        SDL's diagnostics is not ours to do on the user's behalf.
        THE PARSER VALIDATES EVERYTHING BEFORE APPLYING ANYTHING. A rejected spec
        is a NO-OP. Parsing straight into SDL_SetLogPriority would leave the good
        first entry applied after a bad third one, and the user would have a
        configuration they did not ask for and cannot see.
        THE FILE SINK CHAINS rather than replaces: SDL_GetLogOutputFunction before
        SDL_SetLogOutputFunction, and call the previous one. "Also log to a file"
        must not silently cost you your console. SDL holds a mutex across the
        hook, so it is thread-safe for free. SDL FILTERS BEFORE THE HOOK, so a
        message below its level never reaches the sink — the file and the level
        are independent controls.
  assertions: AN ASSERTION IS NOT AN ERROR, and one question separates them:
        COULD A CORRECT PROGRAM, ON A WORKING MACHINE, ENCOUNTER THIS?
          yes -> an ERROR. The world did it to you. Return it; it ships.
          no  -> an ASSERTION. Your own code is wrong. Stop; it may be compiled out.
        THREE MACROS:
          ENGINE_ASSERT(c)  debug only. The default. NO SIDE EFFECTS in c.
          ENGINE_CHECK(c)   every build. Only where continuing is worse than stopping.
          ENGINE_VERIFY(e)  the expression ALWAYS runs; the check is debug only.
        SDL DECIDES ITS LEVEL FROM __OPTIMIZE__, NOT NDEBUG (SDL_assert.h). So -O2
        ALONE takes you to SDL_ASSERT_LEVEL 1 — SDL_assert disabled,
        SDL_assert_release live — with no -DNDEBUG anywhere. OUR log floor keys off
        NDEBUG. TWO GATES, TWO SWITCHES: `-O2` gives dead assertions + live trace
        logging; `-O0 -DNDEBUG` gives the exact inverse. Neither is what you guess.
        SDL_disabled_assert WRAPS THE CONDITION IN sizeof — compiled, never
        evaluated. Good (the condition cannot rot, and `assert(fp = fopen(...))`
        still does not open the file) and a trap (this is why ENGINE_VERIFY exists).
        SDL_enabled_assert IS A `while (!(condition))` LOOP, so SDL_ASSERTION_RETRY
        genuinely RE-TESTS: fix state in a debugger, continue, proceed as though the
        bug had not happened. ALWAYS_IGNORE LATCHES in a static per expansion site.
        ASSERTIONS ARE TESTABLE: SDL_SetAssertionHandler returning IGNORE, plus
        SDL_GetAssertionReport()'s linked list (condition text, file, line,
        trigger_count). verify_53 §F proves an assertion fires on the input that
        should fire it — the thing everyone assumes is impossible.
  errors: THE ENGINE HAD ALREADY CONVERGED ON THE ANSWER TWICE. obj_report (3.5)
        and gpu_report (4.2) are both: a STATUS enum naming the failure precisely,
        the FACTS you ask for next, and a `bool ok()`. Written a module apart by
        nobody trying to match the other. 5.3 NAMES that shape rather than
        inventing a fourth, and converts the odd one out (image_status ->
        image_report).
        THE FOUR RULES:
          1. status + report + ok() when a caller might BRANCH on the failure, or
             when there are diagnostics worth having on SUCCESS too.
          2. A BARE `bool` IS HONEST WHEN THERE IS NOTHING MORE TO SAY
             (platform::set_vsync: one failure mode, one sane response).
          3. PER-SUBSYSTEM status enums, NOT one engine-wide one. A global enum
             becomes a junk drawer where bad_format means six things. The SHAPE is
             unified; the vocabularies are not, and should not be.
          4. "returns false and has ALREADY logged" is retired as an unwritten
             contract — it is now the documented who-logs rule above.
        A REPORT DESCRIBES WHAT IT REFUSED: image_report fills in width/height
        BEFORE the size check, so `too_large` says how large. A report that
        describes the thing it rejected is a diagnosis; one that does not is a
        complaint.
        std::expected IS C++23 AND WE ARE NOT ADOPTING IT YET. Reason recorded:
        the engine's loaders return FACTS, not just values, and they return them
        whether or not they worked — expected has nowhere to put obj_report's
        twelve statistics. Exercise 9.5 builds one anyway. ADOPT A NEW ABSTRACTION
        WHEN THE OLD ONE HAS FAILED YOU, NOT WHEN THE NEW ONE IS ELEGANT.
  program-entry: A PROGRAM PICKS ONE OF TWO ARRANGEMENTS AND THE ENGINE SUPPORTS
        BOTH. Library: construct engine::platform, write your own main() and your
        own while(plat.running()). Framework: derive from engine::app, override
        the hooks, and write ENGINE_MAIN(YourApp) — no main(), no loop.
        engine::app IS IMPLEMENTED ON engine::platform, NEVER BESIDE IT. Anything
        app can do must be reachable from the library path, or the library path
        has quietly become second-class. verify_52 §E renders six frames down each
        path and compares the framebuffers byte for byte.
        <engine/platform/main.hpp> GOES IN EXACTLY ONE .cpp PER PROGRAM, AND THAT
        FILE MUST NOT DEFINE main(). It defines SDL_MAIN_USE_CALLBACKS and
        includes <SDL3/SDL_main.h>, which emits a NON-INLINE definition of
        SDL_main() plus the platform entry point. Two inclusions = duplicate
        symbol _main. That is the ODR, not an SDL quirk — and it is why the entry
        point cannot live in libengine.a: an entry point is not a library's to own.
        It is DELIBERATELY ABSENT from engine.hpp, the umbrella: "include
        everything, it is harmless" must not be a way to acquire a main().
        THE FOUR SDL3 SIGNATURES, verified against SDL 3.4.12:
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppInit   (void **appstate, int argc, char *argv[]);
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppIterate(void *appstate);
          SDL_AppResult SDL_AppEvent  (void *appstate, SDL_Event *event);
          void          SDL_AppQuit   (void *appstate, SDL_AppResult result);
        Returns: SDL_APP_CONTINUE / SDL_APP_SUCCESS / SDL_APP_FAILURE.
  surfaces: THE SURFACE IS CHOSEN BEFORE ANYTHING EXISTS, in app_config, because
        by the time you could regret it the window already belongs to somebody.
          surface::renderer  SDL_Renderer + streaming texture (Module 1's path)
          surface::gpu       a window claimed by NOBODY, for gpu_device::create
          surface::headless  no window, and NO SDL_INIT_VIDEO — runs with no display
        This is Lesson 4.2's "a window is claimed by an SDL_GPU device or driven by
        an SDL_Renderer, never both" turned from a comment into an enum.
        HEADLESS DOES NOT INITIALISE VIDEO, and that is the point rather than an
        optimisation: SDL_Init(SDL_INIT_VIDEO) FAILS on a build server. The
        pre-5.2 `sandbox --shot` called it and never used it, so the
        characterization test built specifically for automation could not run
        anywhere automatic. verify_52 §B pins it (SDL_WasInit == 0).
  edges-and-levels: EDGES BELONG TO THE FRAME, LEVELS BELONG TO THE STEP.
        on_fixed_step runs 0..N times per frame (measured: 0,1,2,1,2 across five
        frames within 1.6 ms of each other at 60 Hz), so an edge query inside it
        fires TWICE on a two-step frame. key_down() in a step is safe — input is
        frame-coherent since Lesson 1.2, so every step in one frame sees the same
        snapshot. key_pressed() is not. Discrete presses go in on_event, where
        event.key.repeat is also available.
  resource-names: NAME EVERY GPU RESOURCE AT CREATION, through
        SDL_PROP_GPU_{BUFFER,TEXTURE,TRANSFERBUFFER}_CREATE_NAME_STRING — never
        through SDL_SetGPU{Buffer,Texture}Name. SDL's own docs on the setter:
        "You should use SDL_PROP_GPU_BUFFER_CREATE_NAME_STRING with
        SDL_CreateGPUBuffer instead of this function to avoid thread safety
        issues", and "This function is not thread safe".
        LESSON 4.3's COMMENT ABOUT THIS WAS WRONG AND IS NOW CORRECTED IN PLACE.
        It claimed the creation property existed for shaders "because a shader is
        immutable the moment it exists", inferring a reason from the asymmetry
        with the buffer/texture setters. There was no asymmetry to explain: the
        property is the recommended path for all three and the setters are the
        older API. A CONFIDENT EXPLANATION OF WHY SOMEBODY ELSE'S API IS SHAPED
        THE WAY IT IS, IS A HYPOTHESIS — and the cheapest place to test it is the
        documentation of the function you are already calling.
        NEITHER API HAS A GETTER. A name is write-only from the program's side, so
        it cannot be asserted on in a harness; only a capture shows it.
        engine::create_named_{buffer,texture,transfer_buffer} in gfx/gpu_debug.hpp
        are the wrappers. A failed property allocation costs the NAME and never
        the RESOURCE — a debugging aid must not be able to break what it aids.
        Cost: 0.0011 -> 0.0018 ms per buffer, paid once, at load.
  debug-groups: A DEBUG GROUP IS A C++ SCOPE, engine::debug_group, and it is
        NEITHER COPYABLE NOR MOVABLE. That is SDL's Metal rule expressed in the
        type system: "On some backends (e.g. Metal), pushing a debug group during
        a render/blit/compute pass will create a group that is scoped to the
        native pass rather than the command buffer. For best results, if you push
        a debug group during a pass, always pop it in the same pass."
        The engine pushes FOUR a frame (upload / clear / blit / scene) and opens
        the scene's group OUTSIDE its pass, closing after it — legal everywhere.
        ON D3D12 ALL THREE CALLS NEED WinPixEventRuntime.dll in PATH or beside the
        executable, and without it they are INERT, not an error: a capture with no
        tree and no diagnostic.
        Measured at ~183 ns per push+label+pop, so four a frame is 0.73 us =
        0.004% of a 16.7 ms frame. THEY SHIP ON.
  measure-the-noise-floor: BEFORE COMPARING TWO TIMINGS, MEASURE THE SPREAD OF THE
        IDENTICAL WORKLOAD RUN SEVERAL TIMES, and require any claimed effect to
        beat it by a factor of TWO. verify_49 §C runs the same frame five times
        and takes the range of the medians (2.46 us here).
        THE FIRST VERSION SKIPPED THIS AND REPORTED A NEGATIVE COST FOR ADDING
        WORK (-0.0003 ms for adding a debug group), which is the tell that a
        measurement has nothing to say. TWO runs was also not enough — a single
        difference is itself a sample of a noisy quantity, and the two-run version
        let -541.7 ns through the guard.
        WHEN AN EFFECT IS BELOW THE FLOOR, SCALE THE WORKLOAD UNTIL IT CLEARS IT
        AND DIVIDE: 1 and 8 groups are unmeasurable, 32 and 128 give 183.6 and
        182.0 ns, and THE AGREEMENT BETWEEN TWO INDEPENDENT ESTIMATES IS THE
        EVIDENCE. Same trick 3.10 used on a profiler zone reading 0.00 us.
  frame-log: THE ENGINE CAN PRINT ITS OWN COMMAND STREAM — engine::frame_log,
        armed by [P] for ONE frame, or by `engine --trace` which prints one frame
        and exits (the headless/CI mode). 33 events, 4 groups, 3 draws, 560
        uniform bytes for 4.8's scene.
        RECORDED FROM THE STATEMENTS THAT ISSUE THE CALLS, never from a parallel
        description of what render() is believed to do. Instrumentation that can
        drift from the code it describes is worse than none, BECAUSE IT IS
        BELIEVED. verify_49 §D asserts the log's draws / uniform bytes / triangles
        against draw_stats — two counters incremented from the same statements,
        which is a test A CAPTURE CANNOT GIVE YOU (a capture is a picture of what
        happened and has no independent account of what should have).
        Fixed-size name buffer (48 B) and fixed event capacity, because the log
        runs inside the frame it measures and an allocation per event would make
        the measured thing differ from the shipped thing. SDL_strlcpy, which
        always null-terminates; strncpy does not.
        WHAT IT CANNOT DO, and why the tools exist: resource CONTENTS at an event,
        replay-to-a-draw, live pipeline state, shader disassembly, per-draw GPU
        timing, per-fragment stepping. All of those need the frame REPLAYED, which
        needs the driver.
  frame-debuggers: RENDERDOC DOES NOT SUPPORT METAL. Its front page: "available
        for Vulkan, D3D11, D3D12, OpenGL, and OpenGL ES development on Windows,
        Linux, Android, and Nintendo Switch". SDL_GPU picks Metal on macOS, so a
        Mac reader uses XCODE'S METAL DEBUGGER — Debug > Debug Executable…, set
        "GPU Frame Capture" to "Metal" in the scheme's Options, run, click the
        Metal icon. No Xcode PROJECT is needed. SDL_gpu.h has a "Debugging"
        section that says all of this and is the first place to look.
        PIX is D3D12-only and is the better GPU PROFILER; RenderDoc is the better
        STATE INSPECTOR. Module 8 returns to that split.
        HOW A CAPTURE WORKS, and it explains every limit: the tool records the
        full CONTENTS of every resource at frame start plus the ordered call list,
        then REPLAYS. That is why it can show any resource at any event, why
        captures are huge, and why the tool must sit at the driver level.
        FOUR QUESTIONS TO ASK ONE: is my draw there at all (missing = CPU-side;
        present but invisible = culled/clipped/depth-rejected); is the right thing
        bound; are the bytes what I think; where did the geometry go. PREDICT
        BEFORE LOOKING — browsing without a prediction generates the feeling of
        investigating and no information.
  vertex-reuse: THE TRUE VERTEX-SHADER INVOCATION COUNT IS NOT MEASURABLE ON THIS
        MACHINE and 4.5's promise that "4.9's RenderDoc capture is where the real
        number finally shows up" IS NOT KEPT. Said plainly in the lesson rather
        than fabricated. Read it from: RenderDoc's Pipeline State statistics or
        VK_QUERY_TYPE_PIPELINE_STATISTICS / VERTEX_SHADER_INVOCATIONS; D3D12's
        D3D12_QUERY_TYPE_PIPELINE_STATISTICS -> VSInvocations; Xcode's Metal
        Debugger per-pipeline counters. Exercise 4.9.5.
        A MODEL INSTEAD, with assumptions stated: a FIFO post-transform cache
        simulated over torus.obj's own index order. best 1225, worst 6912, and the
        curve is a STAIRCASE — 6 through 32 all give 2400, 48 gives 2306, 50+
        gives 1225 — because make_torus emits ring by ring, so reuse happens at
        two distances (a few positions, and ~2*24) and nothing in between.
        THE FINDING IS BIGGER THAN THE QUESTION: REUSE IS A PROPERTY OF THE INDEX
        ORDER. Control: the same 2,304 triangles shuffled go from 2,400 to 6,784
        invocations at cache 32, 2.83x worse, with nothing about the geometry
        changed. This is why real pipelines run an index optimiser (Forsyth,
        meshoptimizer) as a build step.
        The model assumes FIFO (may be LRU), whole vertices (may be fixed-size
        outputs), strictly in-order indices (hardware batches and may reorder),
        and one cache (usually several).
  validation-cost: 1.17x TO RECORD a 3-draw frame (0.0267 vs 0.0228 ms), which
        pays off the promise main.cpp has carried since 4.2. MEASURED ON THE
        RECORDING, not the whole frame, because validation runs on the CPU as each
        call is recorded and a whole-frame number would dilute it with GPU time.
        DO NOT READ 1.17x AS "VALIDATION IS CHEAP": the cost is PER API CALL, so
        it scales with how chatty the frame is. The useful form is "about X per
        call, and I know how many calls I make" — and the frame log counts them.
        Keep it ON while developing; 4.2's argument stands.
  draw-rates: FOUR RATES OF CHANGE, and data is grouped by RATE rather than by
        subject. per FRAME — camera_uniforms (64 B, vertex slot 0) and
        scene_light_uniforms (64 B, fragment slot 0), pushed once before the pass.
        per DRAW — object_uniforms (112 B, vertex slot 1) and material_uniforms
        (32 B, fragment slot 1), pushed BETWEEN draws; SDL licenses this in one
        sentence ("Subsequent draw calls in this command buffer will use this
        uniform data"). per INSTANCE — a vertex buffer at INSTANCE rate (4.5).
        per VERTEX — a vertex buffer at VERTEX rate (4.5).
        The camera and the lamp share a push because both change once a frame,
        not because they are related. Grouping by subject is how you end up
        re-pushing something 1,225 times.
        Measured: a three-object scene moves 128 + 3*144 = 560 bytes of uniform
        traffic a frame, and would move the same 560 if each object had a million
        triangles. PER-DRAW OVERHEAD SCALES WITH OBJECT COUNT, NOT OBJECT SIZE.
  one-draw-per-object: A MATERIAL AND A CULL MODE CANNOT RIDE ON A TRIANGLE. 3.8
        flagged raster_triangle::surface in writing as a cheat that would not
        survive Module 4; this is where the bill arrives. A GPU draw is a LAUNCH,
        not a loop — thousands of fragments enter at once with no moment between
        triangle 7 and triangle 8 — so anything constant across a draw is fixed
        BEFORE it: as pipeline state (cull, fill, depth op, blend, the shaders) or
        as a uniform push (the numbers). Four objects is four draws.
        surface_style is the enum for the part that cannot be a number: solid
        (cull back), two_sided (cull none), wireframe (fill LINE). Three values,
        three pipeline objects at startup. THREE AND NOT MORE because every extra
        axis MULTIPLIES: 2 cull x 2 fill x 2 depth is 8.
        render() DOES NOT SORT. Sorting has several right answers that conflict
        (by pipeline, front-to-back for early-z, back-to-front for transparency,
        by distance for LOD) and the choice belongs to whoever knows what the
        frame is for. It COUNTS instead: pipeline_binds against
        ideal_pipeline_binds. Measured 3 vs 2 on a three-object, two-style scene.
  normals-are-data: A VERTEX SHADER SEES ONE VERTEX, so it cannot compute a face
        normal. collect_triangles could and did — cross(b-a, c-a) inside the
        per-triangle loop, whenever normal_at() returned zero. Three of the four
        built-in meshes carry no normals (cube, quad, icosahedron) and neither
        does 3.2's ground plane, so the fallback had to move OUT of the renderer
        and INTO the geometry. engine::with_normals(mesh, normal_style) is that
        step, and it is where every real engine puts it (aiProcess_GenNormals).
        FLAT FORCES UNSHARED VERTICES — one normal per face, and a vertex holds
        one — so a cube's 8 positions become 36 and an icosahedron's 12 become 60,
        with an index buffer of 0,1,2,3,... and no sharing left to express. That
        is 4.5's `expanded` form arriving for a reason other than a comparison.
        SMOOTH keeps the buffers' shape and averages, WEIGHTED BY AREA FOR FREE:
        |cross(b-a, c-a)| IS twice the triangle's area, so accumulating the
        UN-NORMALISED cross products and normalising once at the end weights each
        face by its area at no cost. Normalising per face first is more work AND
        throws the weighting away. Verified on a cube corner: the averaged normal
        dotted with (-1,-1,-1)/sqrt3 is 1.000000.
        GEOMETRY THAT ALREADY HAS NORMALS IS RETURNED UNCHANGED, whatever style is
        asked for. A file's normals are authorship — 3.5's loader rule, one level
        up. torus.obj's pass through bit for bit (verified).
        AND A RUNTIME TOGGLE BECAME A BUILD-TIME DECISION: 3.8's [Q] flat/smooth
        key is now a property of the vertex buffer, and the two options no longer
        share one. First time in this course that moving to the GPU took something
        away.
  matrix-3x3-uniform: A 3x3 CROSSES A CONSTANT BUFFER AS THREE EXPLICIT COLUMNS,
        never as float3x3. Both occupy 48 bytes (each column padded to a 16-byte
        register), so nothing is saved either way — what the matrix type ADDS is a
        dependence on the compiler's matrix-packing default (column_major for DXC;
        row_major if any #pragma pack_matrix upstream says so), with no diagnostic
        when it is not what you assumed. A transposed normal matrix does not crash:
        it lights every non-symmetric object from a slightly wrong direction.
        The shader rebuilds M*v by its definition — c0*v.x + c1*v.y + c2*v.z —
        which is 2.5's "a matrix is where the basis vectors land" as three
        multiply-adds, and cannot be transposed by a flag.
        MEASURED ANYWAY (verify_48 §C, shaders/matrix_probe.frag.hlsl): on this
        toolchain float3x3 WOULD have worked; DXC packs column-major and the
        matrix-typed reading agrees exactly. Still not what we ship. A default
        that happens to be right on the machine you tested is the most expensive
        kind of correctness there is.
  invisible-on-boxes: THE NAIVE NORMAL MATRIX IS INVISIBLE ON AXIS-ALIGNED
        GEOMETRY BY CONSTRUCTION, not merely usually. With linear part R*S and S
        diagonal, naive = R*S and correct = (R*S)^-T = R*S^-1. Feed an axis e_x:
        naive gives s_x*(R e_x), correct gives (1/s_x)*(R e_x) — PARALLEL, and the
        fragment's normalize() throws the length away. A box's model-space normals
        ARE its own axes, so the difference is exactly zero.
        Measured: 0 px change on two non-uniformly scaled boxes. Squash the
        ICOSAHEDRON to (1.7, 0.5, 1.0), whose twenty face normals are not axes, and
        2,160 px change by up to 143 codes. Worked example: n = (0.7071, 0.7071, 0)
        under S = diag(1.7, 0.5, 1.0) gives (0.9594, 0.2822, 0) naive and
        (0.2822, 0.9594, 0) correct — 57.2 degrees apart.
        THE GENERAL RULE IS ABOUT TESTING, NOT NORMALS: a test scene made of crates
        would have reported a clean pass while the renderer was broken. CHOOSE TEST
        GEOMETRY THAT IS ABLE TO FAIL. Same discipline as 3.1's cyclic planks and
        4.5's deliberately wrong vertex pitch.
  cpu-gpu-agreement: WHAT TWO RASTERIZERS CAN AND CANNOT AGREE ON, measured on the
        same scene, same camera, same light and literally the same mesh_data at
        320x180 (verify_48 §D–F).
        THE SHADING EQUATION: engine::shade() against scene.frag.hlsl over 4,096
        fragments x 3 specular models — worst disagreement 1.192e-07, which is
        ONE FLOAT ULP. Two implementations written three modules apart, in two
        languages, on two processors, agreeing to the last bit a float has.
        COVERAGE: 42 px only the CPU drew, 60 px only the GPU did — a one-pixel
        sliver on every silhouette. Same fill rule (centre-in, top-left tie-break)
        at different sub-pixel resolutions: we round corners to whole pixels, the
        hardware snaps to a fixed sub-pixel grid.
        COLOUR: of 2,729 shared pixels, 86.99% byte-identical, 7.88% one code,
        2.35% two, and 2.46% badly wrong — all of the last on silhouettes, where a
        large number is one pixel of coverage difference wearing a disguise.
        THE FLOOR IS ONE CODE, AND IT IS WORST IN THE DARKS. Our to_encoded()'s
        powf and the hardware's _SRGB write are two approximations of a CURVE, not
        two readings of a table. They agree exactly above linear 0.006; below it
        the curve's slope (12.92 near zero) makes a linear value cross a code
